Replace the `lsqr` call with `regularized_inversion` and add a second-derivative operator as a regulariser. I ran this on your setup, and it works.

```python
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, edge=True)

eps = 30.0  # weight on ||D2 x||^2 (see below for how to choose)
xinv = pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
    Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[np.sqrt(eps)], iter_lim=500
)[0]
```

This minimises `||Op x - y||² + eps ||D2 x||²`. Note that `epsRs` multiplies the operator, so the penalty weight is `epsRs²`. That is why I pass `np.sqrt(eps)`. If you'd rather think in terms of `epsRs` directly, pass it as is and skip the `sqrt`.

**Choosing the weight**

Your noise level is known (0.05), so the discrepancy principle fits well. Pick the largest `eps` for which the data residual `||Op x - y||` is about `sigma * sqrt(n)`, which is 0.87 here. Sweeping `eps` on your problem gave:

| eps | residual (target 0.866) | relative model error |
|---|---|---|
| 0.01 | 0.760 | 0.205 |
| 1 | 0.770 | 0.058 |
| 10 | 0.781 | 0.023 |
| 31.6 | 0.786 | 0.020 (best) |
| 100 | 0.798 | 0.026 |
| 316 | 0.858 | 0.049 |
| 1000 | 1.164 | 0.101 |

- **Best error:** the model error is smallest around `eps` of 10 to 100. That is a 10× improvement over the unregularised end of the sweep.
- **Discrepancy principle:** it lands at `eps` of about 300. That is slightly over-smoothed relative to the true error minimum, which is typical.
- **Recommendation:** start with `eps` of about 30. If your noise level is unknown, use an L-curve. Plot `||D2 x||` against the residual over a log sweep and pick the corner.

**Practical notes**

- **Iterations:** the regularised problem is better conditioned, so 500 iterations is more than enough. Stopping `lsqr` early also acts as implicit regularisation. With an explicit penalty, run to convergence so the result depends on `eps` and not on `niter`.
- **Scaling:** the useful `eps` range depends on the scale of `Op` and the sampling of the model. If you change `n` or the kernel, re-run the sweep.
- **Boundaries:** `edge=True` applies the second derivative at the boundary samples too. Drop it if you don't want the edges smoothed.

Use `regularized_inversion` with a `SecondDerivative` operator. It solves `min ||y − Op x||² + eps² ||D₂ x||²`. Pick `eps` with the discrepancy principle, since you know the noise level (0.05).

```python
import numpy as np
import pylops

# ... Op, y, xtrue as before ...
sigma = 0.05
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, dtype="float64")

def invert(eps):
    xinv = pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
        Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[eps], **dict(damp=0, iter_lim=1000)
    )[0]
    return xinv

# Discrepancy principle: choose eps so that ||Op x - y|| ≈ sigma * sqrt(n)
target = sigma * np.sqrt(n)
epss = np.logspace(-3, 1, 25)
xs = [invert(e) for e in epss]
res = np.array([np.linalg.norm(Op @ x - y) for x in xs])
sol = np.array([np.linalg.norm(D2 @ x) for x in xs])

ibest = np.argmin(np.abs(res - target))
eps_best, xreg = epss[ibest], xs[ibest]
print(f"eps = {eps_best:.3g}, error = {np.linalg.norm(xreg - xtrue) / np.linalg.norm(xtrue):.3f}")
```

Notes:

- **Weight:** `eps` multiplies the regulariser inside the norm, so the penalty is `eps² ||D₂x||²`. Sweeping it on a log grid is the right approach.
- **Choosing eps:**
  - The discrepancy principle picks the `eps` where the data residual matches the expected noise norm, `sigma·√n ≈ 0.87`. Too small an `eps` fits the noise, and too large an `eps` over-smooths.
  - If you don't know `sigma`, plot `res` against `sol` on log-log axes (the L-curve) and take the corner.
  - You can also check against `xtrue` here, since it's synthetic.
- **Solver settings:** Your original `niter=300` acts as implicit regularisation, and it is what makes the result noisy or smooth depending on where you stop. With an explicit regulariser, let lsqr run to convergence (`iter_lim` large) so `eps` is the only smoothing knob.
- **Version differences:** Extra keyword arguments are passed to the underlying solver. In scipy's lsqr the iteration limit is `iter_lim`. The exact name and defaults vary between PyLops versions, so check `help(pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion)` if the call complains.
- **Edges:** `SecondDerivative` defaults to `edge=False`, so the boundary samples are unconstrained. Try `edge=True` if you see artifacts at the ends.

I haven't run this, so the `eps` grid may need shifting for your data. The `res` and `sol` arrays show whether the best value is at the edge of the range.

`pylops.optimization.basic.lsqr` only supports a plain Tikhonov `damp`, so it can't take a second-derivative operator. Use `regularized_inversion` instead. It stacks `[Op; eps*D2]` and solves the stacked problem with lsqr:

```python
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, dtype="float64")

eps = 5  # see below
xinv = pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
    Op, y, Regs=[D2], epsRs=[eps], **dict(iter_lim=500)
)[0]
```

This minimises `||Op x - y||² + eps² ||D2 x||²`. Because `eps` is squared, the useful range is much larger than you might expect.

**Choosing the weight.** I ran your example on a log grid of `eps`. The noise level is σ = 0.05, so the expected residual norm is about σ·√n ≈ 0.87.

| eps | residual ‖Op x − y‖ | relative error vs xtrue |
|---|---|---|
| 0.01 | 0.75 | 1.19 (noise-dominated) |
| 0.1 | 0.76 | 0.20 |
| 1 | 0.77 | 0.058 |
| 3 | 0.78 | 0.024 |
| 10 | 0.80 | 0.026 |
| 30 | 1.12 | 0.094 (over-smoothed) |
| 100 | 3.1 | 0.30 |

- **Discrepancy principle:** this is what you can use on real data. Increase `eps` until the residual norm is about σ·√n. Here that happens around `eps ≈ 15–20`, which is slightly over-smoothed.
- **L-curve:** plot `log ||Op x − y||` against `log ||D2 x||` over a log-spaced `eps` sweep and pick the corner. This works when you don't know σ.
- **On this synthetic problem:** the error is lowest at `eps ≈ 3–10`, so `eps = 5` is a good choice. You only know this because `xtrue` is available. On real data, use the discrepancy principle or the L-curve.

The fit residual stays below the noise level for all `eps` up to 10, so a low residual doesn't tell you the model is good. Over-fitting keeps the residual small and only shows up in the error against `xtrue` or in the roughness of the model.

I ran this in a scratch script in `$TMPDIR` and didn't change any files in your project.

Use `regularized_inversion` with a `SecondDerivative` operator, and choose the weight by scanning a range of values. I ran this on your example.

```python
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, edge=True)

sigma = 0.05                        # noise std (known here; estimate it otherwise)
target = sigma * np.sqrt(n)         # expected residual norm

def invert(lam):
    # minimises ||Op x - y||^2 + lam * ||D2 x||^2
    return pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
        Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[np.sqrt(lam)], iter_lim=300)[0]

lams = np.logspace(0, 4, 17)
res = [np.linalg.norm(Op @ invert(l) - y) for l in lams]
lam = lams[np.argmin(np.abs(np.array(res) - target))]   # discrepancy principle
xinv = invert(lam)
```

`epsRs` multiplies the operator, so the penalty weight is `epsRs**2`. That is why the code passes `np.sqrt(lam)`.

**Results from my run**

The table shows the residual norm relative to `sigma*sqrt(n)` and the model error relative to `xtrue`:

| λ | residual / target | model error |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0.89 | 5.8% |
| 10 | 0.90 | 2.3% |
| **30** | 0.91 | **1.9% (best)** |
| 100 | 0.92 | 2.6% |
| 300 | 0.98 | 4.7% |
| 1000 | 1.34 | 10% |
| 10⁴ | 3.6 | 29% |

**How to pick the weight**

- **Scale:** `SecondDerivative` is unnormalised (`x[i-1] - 2x[i] + x[i+1]`), so useful weights are large, roughly 10–300 here. Values like 0.01–1 barely regularise.
- **Discrepancy principle:** this picks the λ where the residual norm is about `sigma*sqrt(n)`. It gave λ ≈ 300 here. That is slightly over-smoothed, because the fit absorbs some of the noise. The best model error was around λ ≈ 30, so treat the discrepancy value as an upper bound and look at the range below it.
- **L-curve:** plot `log ||Op x - y||` against `log ||D2 x||` over the λ scan and take the corner. Use this if you don't know the noise level.
- **Check by eye:** plot `xinv` for two or three λ values around the chosen one. On real data you can't compute the model error, so this check matters.

I only tested this with `xtrue` known, which you won't have on real data. The λ ≈ 30 optimum comes from that comparison. On real data you'd rely on the discrepancy or L-curve choice.

Prompt

My deconvolution with lsqr gives a very noisy model. I'd like to add a smoothing regulariser on the second derivative of the model and pick a sensible weight for it. How should I change this?

import numpy as np
import pylops

n = 300
t = np.arange(-25, 26)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 6.0) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=25)

xx = np.linspace(0, 1, n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 2 * xx) + 0.5 * np.sin(2 * np.pi * 5 * xx)
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)

xinv = pylops.optimization.basic.lsqr(Op, y, niter=300)[0]

The adjoint in `_rmatvec` has the wrong sign, so it returns the negative of the true adjoint.

The forward operator is `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`. Each `x[j]` enters `y[j-1]` with `+1` and `y[j]` with `-1`. The adjoint therefore needs `x[j] = y[j-1] - y[j]`. Your code does the opposite:

```python
x[1:]  -= y   # should be +=
x[:-1] += y   # should be -=
```

That gives `<Op x, y> = -<x, Op^H y>`, which is why `dottest` fails. The two sides have equal magnitude and opposite sign.

The fix:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

Equivalently, `_rmatvec` is `-(D^T)` of your current version. As a sanity check, the dense matrix of `_matvec` has `-1` on the diagonal and `+1` on the superdiagonal. Its transpose has `-1` on the diagonal and `+1` on the subdiagonal, so `x[0] = -y[0]` and `x[-1] = +y[-1]`. The corrected code matches that.

I haven't run this, so re-run your `dottest` call to confirm it passes.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The signs in `_rmatvec` are flipped, so it returns the negative of the adjoint.

Your forward operator is `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`. Its matrix has `-1` on the diagonal and `+1` on the superdiagonal. The adjoint is the transpose, which gives:

- `x[i+1] += y[i]`
- `x[i] -= y[i]`

Your code does the opposite (`x[1:] -= y`, `x[:-1] += y`). That is the adjoint of `x[i] - x[i+1]`, so `<Op x, y>` and `<x, Opᴴ y>` have opposite signs and `dottest` fails.

Fix:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

The bug is a sign error in `_rmatvec`. It computes the negative of the adjoint.

Your forward operator is `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`. Each `y[i]` therefore adds `+y[i]` to `x[i+1]` and `-y[i]` to `x[i]` in the adjoint. Your code does the opposite:

```python
x[1:]  -= y   # should be +=
x[:-1] += y   # should be -=
```

That makes `_rmatvec` compute `-Aᴴy`. Then `<Ax, y>` equals `-<x, _rmatvec(y)>`, so `dottest` fails.

Fixed version:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

Your `dottest(Op, 99, 100, ...)` call is fine. The arguments are `nr` (rows) and `nc` (columns), which match `shape=(n-1, n)`.

The bug is a sign error in `_rmatvec`. It computes the negative of the adjoint, so `dottest` fails.

Your forward operator is `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`. As a matrix, `A[i, i+1] = +1` and `A[i, i] = -1`. The adjoint is the transpose, so `(Aᵀy)[j] = y[j-1] - y[j]`. That means `y` is added to `x[1:]` and subtracted from `x[:-1]`. Your code does the reverse:

```python
x[1:]  -= y   # should be +=
x[:-1] += y   # should be -=
```

Because of that, `<Ax, y>` equals `-<x, Aᵀy>` instead of `<x, Aᵀy>`, and the dot test fails.

Fix:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

The `dottest(Op, 99, 100, ...)` call is fine. The arguments are `nr=99` and `nc=100`, which match `shape=(n-1, n)`. I haven't run the corrected code.

The adjoint has the wrong sign. `_rmatvec` returns `-Dᴴy` instead of `Dᴴy`.

Your forward operator is `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`, so each `y[i]` gets `+1` on `x[i+1]` and `-1` on `x[i]`. The transpose therefore needs:

- `x[i+1] += y[i]`, which is `x[1:] += y`
- `x[i] -= y[i]`, which is `x[:-1] -= y`

Your code does the opposite (`x[1:] -= y`, `x[:-1] += y`). That gives `⟨Dx, y⟩ = -⟨x, _rmatvec(y)⟩`, so the dot test fails. The two sides have equal magnitude and opposite sign.

Fix:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

I haven't run this. The sign error is clear from the algebra, and `dottest` should pass once it's fixed. If you want to confirm, printing the two dot products with `verb=True` should show them equal and opposite before the fix.

A related check is `Op.todense()`. It should show `-1` on the diagonal and `+1` on the superdiagonal, and `Op.H.todense()` should equal its transpose.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The bug is in `_rmatvec`: it has the signs flipped, so it returns the negative of the adjoint.

Each output `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]` depends on two inputs. The adjoint has to send each `y[i]` back to those same two positions with the same coefficients:

- `x[i+1]` contributes `+1`, so the adjoint adds `+y[i]` at position `i+1`.
- `x[i]` contributes `-1`, so the adjoint adds `-y[i]` at position `i`.

Your code does the opposite (`x[1:] -= y`, `x[:-1] += y`). That computes `-Dᵀy`, so `⟨Dx, y⟩ = -⟨x, your_rmatvec(y)⟩`. The two sides of the dot test have the same magnitude and opposite sign, which is why it fails.

Fix:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

Then `dottest(Op, 99, 100, verb=True)` should pass. As a sanity check, the adjoint of a forward difference is a negative backward difference (`-∇ᵀ`), so `_rmatvec` should have the shape `[-y[0], y[0]-y[1], ..., y[n-2]]`.

I haven't run this. The derivation is straightforward, but you can confirm by running the dot test after the change.

Prompt

I wrote a forward-difference operator as a custom PyLops LinearOperator, but dottest fails. What's wrong?

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils import dottest

class ForwardDiff(pylops.LinearOperator):
    """y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i], i = 0..n-2"""
    def __init__(self, n, dtype="float64"):
        super().__init__(dtype=np.dtype(dtype), shape=(n - 1, n))
        self.n = n

    def _matvec(self, x):
        return x[1:] - x[:-1]

    def _rmatvec(self, y):
        x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
        x[1:] -= y
        x[:-1] += y
        return x

Op = ForwardDiff(100)
dottest(Op, 99, 100, verb=True, raiseerror=False)
Graders — what "good" means for this case
answerllm

Ground truth: the forward operator is y[i] = x[i+1] − x[i], so its adjoint is x[:-1] −= y and x[1:] += y. The _rmatvec has the two slices swapped (x[1:] −= y; x[:-1] += y), which is the NEGATIVE of the adjoint: the dot-test gives ⟨Op x, y⟩ = −⟨x, Op^H y⟩ (same magnitude, opposite sign).

PASS only if ALL of these hold:

  • It identifies that _rmatvec has the wrong sign / the slices swapped (the adjoint returned is minus the true one).
  • It gives a corrected _rmatvec equivalent to x[:-1] -= y; x[1:] += y (or x[:-1] = -y; x[1:] += y, or recommends pylops.FirstDerivative(..., kind="forward") with an equivalent correct definition).
  • It does not name dtype, shape or the dottest arguments as the cause.

Otherwise FAIL.
